**New 2026-07-23 (THR-727, hourly pickup) — tooling (M): `__DEBUG.fireAction()` immediately followed by `__DEBUG.tick()` silently loses the action — the React `setGameState` has not committed before the synchronous tick runs.** Verifying Divine Receipt, firing a player action then ticking to resolution produced no receipt: `fireAction` dispatches `setGameState(prev => {...add action})` (a React state update, applied asynchronously/batched), but `__DEBUG.tick(n)` drives `runTick` synchronously off the *current* committed state. In a tight `fireAction(); tick(12)` JS call the action is not yet in `state.unifiedActions` when the tick loop reads it, so it never progresses and never resolves — the fire is lost with zero error. ~40 fired actions across several attempts produced exactly one receipt (the one attempt that happened to `await d.clearTraces()` between fire and tick, which yielded to the event loop and let React flush). | ~25 min of "the phase must be broken" before isolating it to state-commit timing rather than the new phase. The phase was correct throughout. | M | Yes — any browser verification that fires an action via `__DEBUG` and advances via `__DEBUG.tick` in the same JS turn. | **Workaround:** `await new Promise(r=>setTimeout(r,60))` between `fireAction()` and the first `tick()` so React commits the action into GameState; then tick in a loop with a short `await` per iteration if you also need to observe intermediate state. Also: player-fired actions have an RNG-rolled step duration (`mulberry32(seed + tick*43)`), so a 1-step action may still take several ticks — poll `listPlayerReceipts()` across ~15 single ticks rather than one big `tick(N)`. **Method note:** a receipt/aftermath that "never fires" in `__DEBUG` verification is a state-flush suspect before it is an engine suspect — confirm the action reached `state.unifiedActions` (or emitted an `agent_action_resolved` trace in a *fresh* buffer) before touching the producing phase. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-727 browser verification.

**New 2026-07-24 (THR-722, hourly pickup) — process (S): a retirement ticket's migration target can itself be dead, so "migrate X onto Y" needs a liveness check on Y before the interface-map row is written.** THR-722 specified migrating the dead edge `grants[]` onto `grantsTraitWhileHeld` or `effects[]`. `trait_grant` is the right substrate structurally (~20 catalog entries, a correct resolver collecting `grantedTraits`), but `hasGrantedTrait` has **zero production callers** and `resolveEffects().grantedTraits` is asserted only in tests — so the migration relocates the leak instead of closing it. Taking the ticket at face value would have produced a green-looking row for a contract still consuming nothing. **Workaround:** before writing a post-migration interface-map row, grep the *consumer* side of the target mechanism, not just its writers and resolver; badge LEAKED with a deferral (THR-737 here) when the read site is absent. The downgrade-only invariant in `scripts/interface-contracts.ts` is what makes this the cheap path — LIVE is never mechanical.

**New 2026-07-24 (THR-722, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): `doomIdentityMilestones.test.ts` "milestone triggered flag persists" times out at the default 5s under full-suite load but passes in ~2.1s in isolation.** It calls `initializeGameState` without the raised `MILESTONE_SMOKE_TIMEOUT_MS` its sibling test uses, so it is timing-sensitive rather than broken — the THR-489 red-baseline class. Cost a stash-and-rerun cycle to prove it pre-existing (identical `1 failed | 12981 passed` on a clean tree). **Workaround:** when a suite-only failure looks change-adjacent, `git stash push -u` and re-run the full suite before investigating; do not attribute it without that A/B. Real fix is giving the test the same explicit timeout as its sibling.

**Resolved 2026-07-24 (next hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, resuming THR-722).** The flake was not cosmetic: it is the *only* thing that failed CI on PR #779, so the armed auto-merge could never fire and THR-722 sat shipped-but-unmergeable. Fixed by passing `MILESTONE_SMOKE_TIMEOUT_MS` (already defined in the file) to the second `it`, pushed onto the PR branch. Full suite then went **866 files / 12982 tests, 0 failures** — the first fully-green local suite recorded in this log, which means the "1 known pre-existing failure" baseline everyone had been quoting *was* this flake and nothing else.

**Two lessons worth more than the one-line fix.** (1) **This is a recurrence of #142** — same file, same root cause, 6 weeks apart. THR-293's fix added `MILESTONE_SMOKE_TIMEOUT_MS` to the `it.each` block and left the plain `it` below it on the 5s default; that second test runs **40 ticks** (30 + 10) versus the parametrised one's 30, so the *slower* test was the one left unguarded. When adding a timeout to one test in a file, grep the whole file for sibling `it(`/`it.each(` calls doing comparable work. (2) **"Pre-existing, not mine" is a correct diagnosis but an incomplete action.** The prior run proved pre-existence rigorously and then shipped anyway with auto-merge armed — which cannot merge past a red required check, so the ticket silently stalled for an hour. If a pre-existing failure blocks *your* merge gate, fixing it is in scope; a clean A/B is the start of the job, not the end of it. | THR-722 resume, worktree `quirky-raman-5a4f75`.

**New 2026-07-24 (THR-718, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): proving an engine read-path wired in the browser needs a direct live-`WorldGraph` walk; the `__DEBUG` attachment helpers are too lossy for it.** Verifying that `stat_contribution` items feed `computeRawScore`, two `__DEBUG` helpers got in the way: (1) `spawnAttachment(agent, name)` matches by **partial name and takes the first hit** — `'Ashenmane'` spawned *Ashenmane Horse* (a mount I hadn't migrated) instead of *Ashenmane's Fang*, and `'Fang'` grabbed *Hollowfang*; only fully-unique names (`'Whispering Eye'`, `'Uncut Ruby'`, `'Burned Codex'`) spawned the intended item. (2) `getAgentAttachments(agent)` returned an object whose `Object.keys()` was empty right after spawning, so a scan over it found nothing. Neither exposes `computeRawScore` directly, so the "does the item actually move the tier" question can't be answered from the documented bridge alone. | ~15 min across several spawn attempts. | S | Yes — any UI/engine ticket whose browser proof is "item/edge X flows into read-path Y". | **Workaround that worked:** walk the React fiber to the live `WorldGraph` (`root[__reactContainer…]` → DFS `memoizedProps/State` for an object with `getOutgoingEdges` **and** a populated `nodes` Map — there are decoy empty graph instances, so gate on `nodes.size`), find the agent in `graph.nodes.values()`, then read `getOutgoingEdges(id,'possesses'|'bonded_to')` → target `.properties.effects` and assert the `stat_contribution` payload is present with the authored magnitudes. This proves the data is on exactly the edges the engine walks, which + the unit tests covering the walk = end-to-end wiring, without needing `computeRawScore` in browser scope. **Durable note:** spawn by a fully-unique catalog name (not a shared prefix) and verify the spawned `nodeName` in the return before trusting it; prefer the live-graph walk over `getAgentAttachments` when you need the raw `effects[]`. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-718 browser verification.

**New 2026-07-24 (THR-718 resume, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): resuming a stuck armed PR whose branch lives in a *different* worktree collides with the THR-685 write-guard.** PR #797 (THR-718) was shipped-but-unmergeable — red required check (`check:wiki-freshness:blocking`) + CONFLICTING after main moved. The branch `claude/lucid-lamarr-0e6311` was checked out in a now-dead session's worktree; my session worktree was a different one. Editing the two flagged wiki pages *in that other worktree* was rejected by `worktree-write-guard.sh` — the guard gates on the session CWD and treats any path outside it (including a sibling worktree) as "the home tree", and its suggested "corrected path" was a nonsensical nested path. Git also blocks a second checkout of the same branch. | ~5 min. | S | Yes — every resume-and-fix of a stuck PR whose branch is checked out elsewhere. | **Workaround that worked:** bring the PR branch *into your own* session worktree — `git fetch origin <pr-branch>` then `git checkout -B <local> origin/<pr-branch>`, do the merge/edits/verify there (guard now permits them), and push back with a refspec `git push origin <local>:<pr-branch>`. Push does not need the branch checked out, so the dead worktree is never touched. Also: the two wiki pages are *generated* artifacts (`generate-design-wiki`) but body-preserving — hand-edits survive regeneration; `check:generated-freshness` "regenerating changed it" only means the edited page isn't committed yet (it diffs working-tree-after-prebuild vs committed HEAD), so the fix is simply to commit. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-718 PR-unstick.

**New 2026-07-24 (THR-721, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): `mklink /J` invoked through Git Bash mangles the junction target into `C:\C:\...`.** The session's designated worktree (`serene-jackson-b4a8a4`) shipped with an empty `node_modules`, so the standard fix is to junction it to the healthy home install (per the fresh-worktree memory note). `cmd //c "mklink /J node_modules \"C:\Users\chris\...\node_modules\""` from the Bash tool "succeeded" but produced a junction pointing at `\C:\C:\Users\...` — Git Bash's MSYS path conversion prepended a stray drive/slash. The junction listed as `<JUNCTION> node_modules [C:\C:\Users\...]` and every read through it (`ls`, `test -f`) failed. | ~8 min chasing why vitest was "still missing" after a reported-success mklink. | S | Yes — any session that needs to junction a fresh worktree's `node_modules` from the Bash tool. | **Workaround that worked:** create the junction from the **PowerShell tool** instead — `New-Item -ItemType Junction -Path <wt>\node_modules -Target <home>\node_modules` takes literal Windows paths with no MSYS mangling and resolved correctly on the first try. Verify with `Test-Path "<wt>\node_modules\.bin\vitest.cmd"` before trusting it. Closeout: remove the junction with `cmd //c "rmdir node_modules"` (reparse point only) so the reaper's `git worktree remove --force` can never follow it into the home install. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-721 worktree setup.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-74 PR 3, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): `__DEBUG.gotoAgent()` fires an interrupt beat modal (THE GOD'S WILL / THE FIRST THREAD), which then blocks a clean HexMapV2 screenshot.** Browser-verifying the company cluster on the map needed the camera zoomed to a company's hex. `gotoAgent(id)` zooms the camera *and* — for a threaded/First agent — opens the scry/beat interrupt modal (an intervention-choice overlay), which is not Escape-dismissable and re-presents a fresh card deck when a card is clicked. Two clicks in, the map was still fully obscured. | ~15 min chasing a clean map shot. | S | Yes — any HexMapV2 browser-verify that needs the camera centered on a specific agent/hex. | **Workaround that worked:** don't use `gotoAgent` for a screenshot setup. For the *cluster* proof, `snapshotScene().companyClusters` + `layersActive.includes('companyClusters')` is the sanctioned WebGL assertion (the viewport contract says canvas is asserted via `__DEBUG.snapshotScene()`, not pixels). For a *DOM* surface that's normally hard to reach (the AgentProfileModal Company section, which only opens on click for threaded agents), the mount-overlay recipe (`reference_component_mount_overlay_verify`) is cleaner and click-modal-free: fiber-walk the live `gameState`, build the real card with `getAgentInfoCard`, `createRoot`-render `OverviewTab` into a fixed overlay, screenshot. Note the Browser pane (mcp__Claude_Browser) cannot screenshot headlessly ("pane not displayed, not compositing") — do the render+screenshot in **Claude-in-Chrome**, which can composite; import Vite deps by their resolved URLs (`/node_modules/.vite/deps/react.js`, `react-dom_client.js`) since bare specifiers don't resolve in page context. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-74 PR 3 browser verification.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-719, hourly pickup) — process (M): a plan's *predicted* payload-kind set was wrong in both directions, and only enumerating the real content caught it.** The THR-719 plan named four payload kinds to add (`condition_grant`, `self_remove`, `possession_grant`, `relationship_delta`) while explicitly instructing the executor to port “only the kinds authored content actually uses.” Enumerating the nine shipped `onUseTriggers` blocks showed `possession_grant` and `relationship_delta` had **zero** authored uses, and `condition_remove` — absent from the plan — had **two** (Amber Phial, Herb Bundle). Implementing the plan's list verbatim would have shipped two dead vocabulary entries (the disease the ticket exists to cure) and dropped two working triggers. | ~10 min. | M | Yes — any port where the plan enumerates a target vocabulary from a sample rather than a sweep. | **Workaround:** treat a plan's enumerated set as a hypothesis, not a spec, whenever the plan itself says “derived from authored usage” — grep the full content set *before* writing the type, and record the delta in the PR body. The plan had flagged this as a grey zone (“adjust if the port reveals better shapes”), which is exactly what made it cheap to fix. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-719.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-719, hourly pickup) — engineering (M): two condition-duration fields exist on `has_trait` edges and only one is read; the aftermath path writes the *inert* one.** `decayConditions` (`src/engine/conditionDecay.ts`) counts down `edge.properties.ticksRemaining`. The canonical `apply_condition` aftermath effect (`encounterAftermath.ts:1531`) writes `durationTicks`, which a repo-wide grep shows has **zero** `has_trait`-edge readers. Mirroring the aftermath shape in the new `action_trigger` applier — the obvious “match the existing machinery” move, and what the plan implied — would have minted conditions that never expire. | ~10 min (caught by porting an integration test that asserted decay rather than deleting it). | M | Yes — any new code path that attaches conditions. | **Workaround:** write `ticksRemaining` for finite durations and omit it for indefinite ones; assert the decay clock in a test rather than asserting the write. **Note the wider finding is unfixed and out of scope here:** conditions applied by encounter aftermath appear never to expire. Filed as THR-761 rather than widened into this ticket. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-719.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-719, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): the CLI's `@hero` alias resolves to the *ascendant*, so on-use item probes attached to it never fire.** Probing the ported triggers with `spawn attachment @hero starter_burned_codex` attached the item successfully (“4 effects: … action_trigger”) but produced no firing across 60 ticks. `@hero` resolved to `asc.archetype.chaos_0` — an ascendant, which does not run mortal encounter resolution, so `action_complete` never fires for it. This looks exactly like “the trigger is broken.” | ~10 min, and it is precisely the Debugging Protocol's “verify the noun before the verb” failure mode. | S | Yes — any CLI probe of mortal-loop behavior. | **Workaround:** attach to a real mortal (`ind_*`) rather than `@hero`, or skip the alias entirely and let the reward system distribute the item organically — the strongest evidence in this ticket came from an unmodified seeded run where the reward pipeline handed out a Burned Codex and its trigger fired on its own. Also note the CLI's `ALL_ATTACHMENT_TEMPLATES` pool covers reward + starter catalogs only, not `anomaly-reward-catalog.ts`. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-719 CLI probe.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-719 resume, hourly pickup) — flaky-test (M): `debugTickBatch.test.ts` "clamps a request above DEBUG_TICK_MAX" times out at its 60s budget under full-suite CI load, blocking an armed PR — the third instance of the "slow guard-rail test starves on a shared runner" class.** PR #830 (THR-719) was shipped, green locally (13104 passed / 0 failures) and armed for auto-merge, but its required `Test · Typecheck · Build` check failed on this one test, so the merge could never fire. The test is **untouched by the PR** — it came from THR-689 (2026-07-21) and runs up to `DEBUG_TICK_MAX` (200) real ticks. Locally it completes in **17.9s** against the 60s budget (a 3.4× margin); on CI, where 880 test files share a runner and the suite's own wall-clock was **987s**, it exceeded 60s outright. `main` was green across the prior 12 runs and this was the only failure in the last 25 runs across all branches, so it is contention-dependent, not systemic. | ~20 min: fetching the CI log, proving the test pre-existing (`git log` on the file + an empty `git diff origin/main...branch` for it), and timing it locally to size the fix. | M | Yes | **Workaround/fix:** raise that test's explicit timeout 60s → 180s (≈10× the measured local runtime), with a comment recording *why* the budget is CI-sized rather than machine-sized. The guard rail keeps its meaning — a genuinely unbounded loop still fails — while surviving parallel load. **Diagnostic shortcut worth reusing:** before attributing a suite failure to your change, run `git diff --stat origin/main...<branch> -- <test-file>`; an empty diff proves the file is untouched far faster than a stash-and-rerun A/B. **This is the same class as #142 / the 2026-07-24 THR-722 entry** (`doomIdentityMilestones.test.ts`), whose lesson applies verbatim and was followed here: *a pre-existing failure that blocks your own merge gate is in scope to fix; a clean pre-existence diagnosis is the start of the job, not the end of it.* Durable fix for the class: tick-running tests should derive their timeout from tick count (e.g. a `TICKS_PER_SECOND_FLOOR` constant) instead of each hard-coding a literal sized on an idle dev machine. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-719 PR-unstick.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-738, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): `gh run view --log` on an `actions/github-script` job echoes the script *source*, so a naive grep for an output string matches the code that would print it, not the print.** Verifying the auto-close decision meant reading what the workflow actually logged. `gh run view <id> --log | grep "No Linear issues"` returned a line — but it was `console.log('No Linear issues referenced — nothing to close');`, the inlined script body, which github-script echoes in full before executing. Every candidate output string (`Issues to close`, `already closed`, `Docs-flush PR`) appears in the source too, so the grep "succeeds" identically whether the branch ran or not — a false-positive shaped exactly like the evidence you were looking for. | ~6 min and two wrong reads; briefly recorded the negative-control result off a source line rather than an output line. | S | Yes — any verification that reads a github-script step's decision from its log (auto-close, flush guard, any inline-JS workflow). | **Workaround that worked:** anchor the grep on the runner's ISO timestamp prefix, which only real output carries: `gh run view <id> --log \| grep -E "Z (Issues to close\|No Linear issues\|✅\|ℹ️)"`. The echoed source lines have no timestamp between the step name and the text. Cheaper still when the workflow's effect is observable in a system of record — here the Linear `stateHistory` array (`endedAt: null` on a single entry) proved "never transitioned" independently of the log, and its `completedAt` matched the ✅ line to the second, which is stronger evidence than either source alone. **General rule: prefer the state the workflow changed over the log of it changing.** | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-738 live verification.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-728, hourly pickup) — engineering (M): a plan's named capability source was off by a factor of ~20 in *units*, and only measuring the outcome distribution caught it.** The plan specified `computeCapability(graph, ascendantId, step.reach)` as the roll input for player casts, flagging as a grey zone "verify it returns a sane value; if it reads inputs the ascendant lacks, wire through `getAscendantDomainAffinities`." It returns **0.018 for every god at every reach** — and wiring affinities in naively does not fix it either. The ascendant node carries `domainAffinities` (2–5), which `rankedAffinityReaches` uses purely to *rank* its two permanent reaches; mortals carry `domainCapabilities` (10–40, from `generateDomainCapabilities`) on the raw scale the sigmoid (`SIGMOID_MIDPOINT = 10`) actually consumes. Same concept, same shape, two different units, no type distinguishing them. Shipping the plan literally would have floored **94%** of casts onto a single outcome band — a *flatter* receipt than the auto-success the ticket exists to replace, and it would have passed every "does it roll?" test. | ~25 min, including one wrong fix that had to be reverted. | M | Yes — any feature that starts consuming a capability/score property on an entity type that never fed that pipeline before. | **Workaround that worked:** measure the *distribution*, not the call. A 400-seed sweep over the real templates printed `{success_at_cost: 374, near_miss: 26}` at raw 0 and a healthy 4-band spread at raw ≈8, which located the cliff in one run and sized the fix; a unit test asserting "capability > 0" or "outcome varies" would have passed at raw 0 and hidden it. **The wrong fix is worth naming:** the obvious move — teach `computeRawScore` to read `domainAffinities` — is a trap, because that score is what THR-613's Deepening tier-crossing beats are tuned against, so it silently re-tiers every ascendant. Correct shape was a caller-supplied bonus (`computeCapabilityWithRawBonus`) confined to the player path, with a test pinning that the shared score is unchanged. **General rule: when two subsystems name the same concept with different property names, assume different units until a measurement says otherwise.** | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-728.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-728, hourly pickup) — engineering (S): `export const FLAG = true` narrows to the literal type `true`, making a one-flag revert switch statically dead and untestable.** The plan's headline safety property was "`PLAYER_CAST_VARIANCE_ENABLED = false` restores the previous behavior verbatim." Declared as `export const PLAYER_CAST_VARIANCE_ENABLED = true`, TypeScript infers the literal type `true`, so `if (!FLAG && …)` is provably-false dead code to the compiler and bundler, and the test asserting the revert path fails to compile (`Argument of type 'false' is not assignable to parameter of type 'true'`). The flag looks present in review and is unreachable in practice. | ~5 min, surfaced by the typecheck ratchet rather than by tests. | S | Yes — every feature flag, kill switch, and one-flag revert declared as a bare `const`. | **Workaround:** annotate the type explicitly — `export const PLAYER_CAST_VARIANCE_ENABLED: boolean = true` — which keeps both branches live and lets a test flip it via `vi.spyOn(mod, 'FLAG', 'get')`. Worth a lint rule if more revert switches land. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-728.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731, hourly pickup) — process (L): the Step 0.6 substrate check proved the *machinery* existed and never asked whether it had any raw material, so a plan passed intent-judge Allow with a trigger that cannot fire.** The THR-731 plan specified a lair raider-band spawner: "a lair at/above `BAND_SPAWN_LAIR_STAGE` with ≥`GROUP_MIN_MEMBERS` living monster-faction members spawns a `faction_band` group." Its Substrate inventory is genuinely rigorous — it verified `createGroup` is composition-agnostic, `faction_band` ships, `phaseLairEscalation` is live, `monsterFactionSeed.ts` writes `monsterFactionId` — and the cold-boot judge independently confirmed all three claims, rating the substrate dimension "exemplary." Every one of those checks was about the *code*. None asked the population question, and the answer is that monster factions have **no `member_of` roster at all** (`seedMonsterFaction` mints an abstract node with a `controls` edge) and a lair mints **exactly one** named elite ever (`createNamedElite`, on the minor→major upgrade) against a `BAND_SIZE_MIN` of 3. Runtime probe, `--seed 42 --map medium` tick 0: **13 lairs, 0 elites.** The trigger could never have fired once. Same plan, same section: `BAND_SPAWN_LAIR_STAGE = 3` names a numeric stage the lair system doesn't have — it gates on `lairTier: 'minor' \| 'major' \| 'legendary'`. | ~35 min to diagnose, re-scope to the guild path, and file the deferral. Cheap only because the probe ran *before* any spawner code was written. | L | Yes — every plan whose feature consumes a population (members, items, encounters, nodes of a kind) rather than only calling functions. | **Workaround:** extend the Step 0.6 evidence bar from "the system exists" to "**the system exists *and* here is a runtime count of the entities it consumes**." One CLI `eval` line per premise noun, pasted into the Substrate inventory beside the grep hits, would have caught this at design time for the same 5 minutes it cost at implementation time. The grep half answers *can this code run*; the count half answers *will it ever have anything to run on*, and only the second distinguishes a live feature from a dormant one. **This is THR-614's failure mode wearing a disguise:** THR-614 green-fielded a system that already existed and sat dormant; here the plan correctly found the existing system and missed that its inputs are empty. Both ship silence. **Note the check that did work:** the same discipline applied one level down caught a second dormancy in this ticket — the guild path's own eligibility predicate, `isGroupEligibleAgent`, requires `spotlightTier === 'spotlight'`, and a probe showed that gate left exactly **one** faction of 49 able to muster, so band eligibility deliberately omits it. A plan-faithful implementation would have shipped a spawner that fires roughly never, passed every unit test, and looked correct in review. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 1.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731, hourly pickup) — engineering (S): gating a probability roll *before* structural eligibility silently scales the effective rate by how much junk the world contains.** `spawnFactionBands` originally rolled `BAND_SPAWN_CHANCE = 0.15` once per faction and only then checked whether the faction could actually field a band — the tidier shape (one rng draw per faction per sweep, stream advance independent of world state, which is the usual determinism instinct). At tick 24 on `--seed 42 --map medium` it passed 5 of 49 factions and **all five were barren**: four had zero eligible members and one had no colocated cluster of three. Zero bands, from a system that was otherwise working. Only ~10 of 49 factions can ever muster, so ~80% of the probability budget was being spent on guilds that worldgen minted empty. | ~15 min, and it presented as "the spawner doesn't work" rather than "the rate is wrong" — indistinguishable from a real bug until the per-faction eligibility numbers were printed. | S | Yes — any per-entity roll over a heterogeneous population where most entities are structurally ineligible. | **Workaround:** check structural eligibility first, roll second, and extract the predicate (`canFieldBand`) so it is independently readable as the answer to "why is this entity not doing the thing?". Determinism is unaffected — the sweep order is id-sorted, so the rng stream is still a pure function of the seed. **Diagnostic worth reusing:** when a rolled system produces nothing, log the *funnel* (candidates → passed roll → passed eligibility → acted), not just the final count; the stage where the number collapses names the bug immediately, and here it distinguished "unlucky seed" from "the budget is aimed at the wrong population." | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 1.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731 PR 2, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): the typecheck ratchet counts errors per *access site*, so reading an already-broken field twice more raises the count by 2 with no new defect.** `resolveContestationPair` sits on four pre-existing baseline errors: `attackerStep.reach` / `.difficulty` on an `ActionStepOrBranch` union that does not declare them (THR-489 red baseline). Adding the group-strength hook needed the step's reach twice more — once to pick the acting member, once for the capability read — which is zero new *defects* but `+2` on `check:typecheck`, and the gate correctly failed the commit. | ~8 min, most of it deciding whether the increase was legitimate and warranted `--update`. | S | Yes — any change that touches a function already sitting on baseline errors, which after THR-489 is most engine code. | **Workaround:** hoist the offending read into a single local (`const attackerReach = attackerStep.reach;`) and use the local everywhere. One access site instead of three, count returns to baseline, and the code reads better anyway. **Do not reach for `--update` first:** the escape hatch exists for a genuine count change, and using it to absorb "I read the same broken field again" quietly ratchets the baseline upward one PR at a time — which is the drift the gate was built to stop. **Do not add a cast either** — `as ActionStep` would *lower* the count by suppressing the pre-existing errors, which is also a baseline change and hides four real defects that belong to THR-489. The neutral move is the right one. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 2.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731 PR 2, hourly pickup) — engineering (S): a test fixture wrote group leadership as a `leaderId` property; the engine reads it from a `commanded_by` edge, so the leader-exclusion assertion failed while everything else passed.** The band-opposition fixture built groups with `properties.leaderId`, which looks right and is what `roster` sits beside. `getGroupLeader` reads `graph.getOutgoingEdges(groupId, 'commanded_by')[0]`. Every hex/pairing test still passed, because `getGroupPosition` falls back to "first member with a position" and `synthesizeBandCounter` falls back to the id-sorted first member — both fail-soft paths doing exactly their job. The single test that could see the difference was the casualty rule "never take the leader while anyone else stands," which picked the leader and failed. | ~10 min. | S | Yes — any test fixture for a subsystem whose relationships are edges. | **Workaround:** build the edge, not the property. The deeper point is the repo's own load-bearing rule — *relationships between entities are graph edges, never string ID fields in a property bag* — applied to fixtures as well as to production code: a fixture that encodes a relationship as a property is testing a world shape the engine never produces. **The failure mode worth noting is how well it hid:** good fail-soft coverage meant one assertion out of 22 caught it, and if the casualty rule had not existed the suite would have been green against a fixture with no leader at all. When a group-shaped fixture passes, check that the queries you are *not* asserting on can still find what they need. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 2.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731 PR 3, hourly pickup) — engineering (M): content travelled two draw paths and only one enforced its eligibility, so party-exclusive templates were reachable by exactly the actors they declared themselves off-limits to.** THR-74 shipped party-exclusive delves with `actorAffinities: ['group']` + `minGroupMembers: 2` and a source comment stating they are "unreachable to solo agents by construction," and it made that true in `generateUnifiedCandidates`. But templates authored in `encounter-content.ts` also reach agents by a second, entirely separate road: `getEncountersByLocationType` → `encounterCache` (built per *location*, actor-independent) → `encounterFilterPipeline` (per *agent*, five stages: awareness, visibility, prerequisites, threat, cap). **Not one of those five stages reads `actorAffinities`.** Measured at implementation time: `getEncountersByLocationType("ruins")` returned 7 group-exclusive templates — `sunken_vault`, `broken_span`, `hollow_watch`, plus PR 2's two band counters — with nothing downstream gating them. The acute case was mine: a `requiresOpposingBand` confrontation would have surfaced with no band to confront, an encounter about a specific enemy played against an empty chair. | ~25 min to find, ~15 to fix. Found only because the new gate forced the question "which code actually decides whether an agent sees this template?" — the pre-existing half had been live and silent since THR-74. | M | Yes — every future template authored in `encounter-content.ts` with an eligibility claim, and any assumption that a filter proven on one path holds on all of them. | **Workaround:** enforce eligibility in `filterByPrerequisites` (stage 3, whose own docstring called it a "placeholder for future trait prerequisites" — it is the pipeline's designated home for exactly this), resolving both lookups lazily and at most once per agent because the pipeline runs for every agent every tick. **The generalisable lesson is about *where* a gate is verified.** A unit test proving `generateUnifiedCandidates` withholds a template is true and worthless if the template also arrives by another road; the test passes, the content leaks, and nothing is red. Before trusting any content gate, enumerate the paths from the authoring table to the agent — here, grep the exported accessors of the content file (`getEncountersByLocationType`, `getEncountersBySublocationAndLocation`, `getEncountersForLocation`) and check each consumer, rather than reasoning from the one call site you happen to be editing. **Related in kind to the interface-map thesis (THR-717):** the contract "only a company may take this" existed as an authored claim and a comment, and was enforced at one of its two read sites — a leak invisible to file-import tooling because both paths import the same module perfectly legitimately. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 3.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731 PR 4, hourly pickup) — engineering (M): a content template gated on a sublocation id that worldgen never mints, so the ticket's own capstone encounter was unreachable by construction while every gate around it tested green.** `encounter.confront_guild_falls` — the capstone of the whole ticket — and `encounter.band_defend` both declared `sublocationTypes: ['sublocation-type.guildhall']`. Worldgen mints `sublocation-type.guild-hall`, hyphenated: **34 of them on `--seed 42 --map medium`, and zero of the unhyphenated id on any seed.** One missing hyphen, and neither encounter could ever be drawn. Nothing was red: the templates validate, ship, appear in the registry, pass the content-integrity suite, and score normally — they simply never match a location. PR 3's own gate tests were real (they call `getUnifiedTemplateById` on a shipped template) but sampled only `confront_standoff`, which carries no `sublocationTypes` at all, so the family's one sublocation-gated member was never exercised. | ~40 min, and only because PR 3's checkpoint had written down the right next suspect ("if `spawn band` shows the family still cannot draw, the location-subtype sets are the first suspect"). Without that note the natural next move is tuning scores, which would have been weeks of chasing a typo. | M | Yes — every template gating on `sublocationTypes`, and every content field whose values are free-form strings cross-referenced against a generator. | **Workaround:** a derived content-integrity test in `src/data/__tests__/unified-action-templates.test.ts` that fails on any template gating on a sublocation id no generator can mint. **Two things about it are load-bearing and both were learned the hard way.** (1) The mintable set must be *derived*, not listed: it is the union of the four worldgen menus (`REACH_SUBLOCATION_MENU`, `SPHERE_SUBLOCATION_MENU`, `SETTLEMENT_INFRASTRUCTURE`, `SUBTYPE_SUBLOCATION_MAP`) **plus** the runtime `GOLD_SUBLOCATION_SPECS` in `phaseSublocations` — omitting that fifth, emergent source made the first version flag four legitimately-reachable templates (`mine`, `smugglers-den`, `caravan-rest`), which is how a well-meant guard gets deleted as noisy. (2) It must assert over the **merged `locationSubtypes`**, not the authored `sublocationTypes`: `toUnifiedTemplate` folds `locationTypes` and `sublocationTypes` into that single array, so the authored field does not exist on `UnifiedActionTemplate` at all. The first draft read `template.sublocationTypes`, matched **zero** templates, and passed cheerfully with the bug deliberately reintroduced — a green test asserting over an empty set. The shipped version therefore also asserts that it found sublocation-gated templates at all, so it cannot silently go vacuous if the merge shape changes again. **The transferable practice is falsifying your own guard:** reintroduce the defect and watch the test fail before trusting it. Doing that took two minutes and was the only reason the vacuity was caught; a passing new test on a fixed bug proves nothing about the test. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 4.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-731 PR 4, hourly pickup) — tooling (S): a loosely-typed bridge registry cannot accept a precisely-typed function, so annotating a new handler's params trades one ratchet error for a worse one.** `window.__DEBUG`'s encounter bridge is registered as `Record<string, (...args: unknown[]) => unknown>`. Adding `spawnBand` to it produced one net-new typecheck error, and the two obvious fixes each made things worse rather than better: leaving params inferred gives `unknown` where a `string` is needed (`+1`), while annotating them (`factionQuery: string`) makes the whole function signature incompatible with the registry's index type by contravariance (`+1`, and a less informative error). Every sibling — `spawnNpc`, `spawnLocation`, `spawnSublocation`, `spawnAttachment`, `moveAgent` — carries the inferred-`unknown` variant as part of the pre-existing 76-error GameView baseline, so the pattern is established and not obviously wrong. | ~12 min of ratchet ping-pong before recognising the shape of the problem. | S | Yes — any new handler added to `__DEBUG`'s bridge or a similarly-typed registry. | **Workaround:** leave the params inferred (matching the siblings) and cast at the *use* site — `spawnDebugBand(state, factionQuery as string, options as { role?: ... } | undefined)` — which keeps the registry assignment valid and moves the assertion to where the real contract is known. On the implementation side of `debug-bridge.ts`, the fallback object's return type also needs an inline type-only cast (`as import('./engine/debugWorldSpawnTools').DebugWorldSpawnResult`), since `?? { success: false, … }` otherwise widens to `{}` against the declared return type in the `.d.ts`. Net effect: ratchet back to the 3519 baseline with no `--update`. **The reusable read:** when the ratchet moves by one on a new call into old plumbing, the question is not "which annotation silences it" but "where is the type actually known" — and for a stringly-typed registry that is always the call site, never the signature. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-731 PR 4.

**New 2026-07-25 (hourly pickup) — environment (Blocked): GitHub Actions stopped executing jobs on an account-billing failure, which both stranded a finished ticket and silently opened the merge gate. Third recurrence of impediments #91 and #136.** Every `CI` and `Linear Auto-Close` run from 15:05 UTC onward reports `conclusion: failure` within 3–5 seconds having executed **zero** jobs, carrying GitHub's own annotation: *"The job was not started because recent account payments have failed or your spending limit needs to be increased."* Two distinct consequences, and the second is much worse than the first. (1) THR-731's PR #850 merged at 15:05:40Z with the closing keyword correctly line-anchored in both the commit body and the PR body, but the auto-close workflow never ran, so the ticket sat In Dev holding the WIP=1 slot while being entirely finished — exactly the lane-pinning failure mode #136 described. (2) **The required `Test · Typecheck · Build` check does not fail when Actions is down — it records as `skipped`, and a skipped required check satisfies branch protection.** Three commits reached `main` this way during the window (`cb725de8`, `12337a1a`, `51a90d1f`; all benign — one merge, two briefing edits), but for the duration of the outage *any* PR can merge without a single test having run. The protection rule is intact and vacuous at the same time. | Blocked — one hourly run produced no shipped work, and the branch-protection guarantee was silently void for the whole window. | Blocked | Partial. | **Diagnosis:** the disambiguation prescribed after this morning's *retracted* 15:12 call — a startup-failure cluster looks identical to quota exhaustion, so ask whether a **fresh** run executes jobs before escalating — is exactly right and costs one run. Here `gh run rerun 30162937429` reproduced the failure in 3 seconds with the same annotation, which is what separates this from the transient: the 15:12 call inferred a cap from a *signature* and was wrong; this one has GitHub naming the cause and reproducing on demand. **Do not skip the re-run** — it is the whole difference between the two. **Resolution is Christian-only:** restore the account budget under Billing & plans. No agent action can clear it. **Executor posture while it lasts:** do not claim new implementation work — shipping game code into an unguarded `main` trades the project's only real safety net for one hour of throughput, and a healthy queue loses nothing by waiting. Resume the ticket, comment the evidence, unassign to free the WIP slot, exit clean. **After restore:** a startup-failed run is never retriggered by anything, so each stranded ticket needs its auto-close run re-run by hand (for THR-731, run `30162937429`) or the issue closed manually. **Durable fix, now recommended for the third time (#136 proposed it in May and it was never built):** a billing-failure detector on the hourly tick that greps `gh run list` output for the account-payment string, surfaces it as a Christian-facing item, and exits clean — this class is trivially machine-detectable and has now cost three separate sessions the same rediscovery. **RESOLVED 2026-07-25 ~18:00 UTC (next hourly run).** Billing was restored between runs; the proof is a *fresh* run executing jobs, not a signature — THR-732's PR #856 ran `Test · Typecheck · Build` for 17 minutes (17:39:25→17:56:55Z) and passed, versus the 3–5s zero-job deaths. Both consequences were then cleared by the remediation this entry prescribes: PR #856 was armed and merged (`76ab4af6`), and THR-731's stranded auto-close run `30162937429` was re-run by hand and succeeded, moving the ticket to Done at 18:04Z after ~3h pinned In Dev while finished. **Both halves of the prescription held up in practice** — the "re-run a fresh run before escalating" disambiguation, and "a startup-failed run is never retriggered by anything, so re-run each stranded auto-close by hand." **The one refinement for next time:** the *previous* run's decision to leave the PR open and deliberately **unarmed** (rather than arming it into a vacuous branch-protection gate) is what made this run a 4-command cleanup instead of a re-verification — record that posture as the correct one, since arming would have landed untested code and left nothing to distinguish it from a normal ship. The durable fix (a billing detector on the hourly tick) is still unbuilt and is now recommended for the fourth time. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, 2026-07-25 16:00 UTC; THR-731 resume. Resolution: 2026-07-25 18:00 UTC run.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-732, hourly pickup) — engineering (M): the plan's named input for Reunite was a property that dissolution deliberately empties, so the verb would have found nobody to call.** The THR-732 plan specifies Reunite's targets as "living un-grouped **roster** members" of a disbanded company, and its Substrate Honesty table verifies the backing shape as *"Disbanded-company persistence (`groupStatus: 'disbanded'`, `roster`, `disbandedAtTick`) — Yes, `groupQueries.ts`, persisted by `dissolveGroup`."* Both halves of that check are true and the conclusion is still wrong: `dissolveGroup` **sets `roster: []`** on its way out. The property exists on disbanded companies, is documented, is persisted — and is always empty on exactly the input Reunite takes. A faithful implementation returns zero gatherable members for every target, forever, and the failure is silent: the eligibility predicate simply never passes, so the card is never offered and no error is raised. What actually survives dissolution is the **`member_of` edges**, which `dissolveGroup` stamps with `leftAtTick` rather than removing — and `groupDissolution.ts`'s own file header says so in as many words ("that pair is the historical record prose reads from later"). | ~15 min, caught while reading `dissolveGroup` to confirm the window property would not be clobbered — i.e. by reading the producer, not the consumer. | M | Yes — any feature that reads a "historical" property off an entity its own lifecycle code tears down. | **Workaround:** a `getFormerGroupMembers` query that walks *all* incoming `member_of` edges including the closed ones, with `getReunitableMembers` layering the living/ungrouped filter on top; the interface-map row for it names the mechanism as the `leftAtTick` **edge property** written by `dissolveGroup`, not the accessor function, so the contract points at the thing that actually crosses the boundary. **The generalisable check:** Step 0.6 asks *does this substrate exist* — for anything read after an entity's death, the follow-up question is **"what does the teardown path do to it?"** Grepping the property name finds the declaration and the writer and tells you it is real; only reading the *destructor* tells you it is empty when you need it. Both a roster-based and an edge-based implementation pass a naive "returns former members" test written against a live company — the discriminating fixture has to dissolve the group first, which is why the shipped test asserts `roster === []` and the edge-walk returning all three riders **in the same test body**. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-732.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-732, hourly pickup) — engineering (S): a content pool keyed by "sphere" uses a vocabulary the engine never produces, and an invented sphere id crashed an unrelated shipped test.** Authoring Sunder, `sphereAffinity: 'dusk'` looked well-supported: `GROUP_NAME_SPHERE_ADJECTIVES` (`group-name-content.ts`) is keyed `hunger / mind / spirit / blood / stone / tide / flame / dusk`, which reads exactly like a sphere list. It is not one. The engine's spheres are `force / matter / energy / life / mind / spirit / time / entropy` plus the four Foundation spheres — **only `mind` and `spirit` appear in both sets**, so six of that pool's eight keys are unreachable content, silently absorbed by its `?? []` fallback. The invented id surfaced not through any action-template test but through `ascendantBeatPresentation.test.tsx`, which renders every milestone beat and its granted cards: `SPHERE_COLORS['dusk']` returned `undefined` and `tintDark` died on `hex.slice(1)`. Correct value was `entropy` (the Creation sphere `reach: 'shadow'` maps to via `REACH_TO_SPHERE`). | ~10 min. Filed the pool mismatch as THR-770. | S | Yes — any new template picking a `sphereAffinity`, and any content table keyed by an id vocabulary with no compile-time link to its source of truth. | **Workaround:** derive the sphere from the template's reach via `REACH_TO_SPHERE` rather than picking a word that sounds right, and treat neighbouring content tables as **untrusted** evidence of what an id vocabulary contains — a `Record<string, readonly string[]>` keyed by convention has no compile-time link to the real union, so it can drift arbitrarily far and stay green. The authoritative sets here are `SPHERE_COLORS` / `REACH_TO_SPHERE` in `src/components/icons/constants.ts`. **Worth noting how it was caught:** a sweep test that renders every member of a registry (here, every milestone beat *with its granted cards*) turned an invented data value into a hard failure at the UI layer, which no data-shape test would have done — `sphereAffinity` is typed `SphereName`, and `'dusk'` only failed to compile because the union is real; had the field been `string`, the crash would have waited for a player to open the beat modal. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-732.

**New 2026-07-25 (THR-734, hourly pickup) — process (S): a handoff instruction went stale between authoring and pickup, and following it faithfully would have written drift into the artifact that exists to prevent drift.** THR-734's handoff (2026-07-24) split its five terms into "shipped — reconcile against code" (Group, Company, Group Cohesion) and "design-approved, not yet shipped (THR-74 PR 2b) — add with the not-yet-implemented marker" (Draw Together, Bless this Company). That was accurate when written. By pickup, PR 2b had landed: `company.bless` and `company.draw_together` are live `UnifiedActionTemplate`s alongside `company.reunite` / `company.sunder` (THR-732), with constants (`BLESS_COMPANY_COHESION_DELTA`, `DRAW_TOGETHER_RADIUS_HEXES`), graph ops (`bless_company`, `draw_together`), a convergence read in `encounterScoring`, and dedicated tests. A faithful execution would have shipped two UL entries marked `[DESIGN]` for behavior a player can invoke today — and the UL is precisely the artifact whose job is to be right about what exists. | ~10 min to detect and decide; the detection was free because the reconciliation the *other* three terms required surfaced the constants for all five. | S | Yes — every handoff whose ticket depends on a sibling PR that is in flight at authoring time, which is most multi-PR project work. | **Workaround:** treat a handoff's shipped/not-shipped split as a **claim with a timestamp**, not a fact, and re-verify it at pickup for the same one grep it would take to write the entry anyway. The resolution rule that settled it here: the handoff's own governing clause said *"reconciled against shipped code"*, so when a specific sub-instruction contradicts the general instruction, the general one wins and the reversal gets recorded (commit body, PR body, Linear comment) rather than silently applied. **The structural point is which direction the staleness runs.** A handoff can only go stale toward *more* shipped, never less — so the failure is always the same shape (a doc claiming design-only for live behavior) and always caught by the same check: grep the id before you write the marker. **Generalises past docs:** any ticket whose acceptance depends on "X is not yet built" is asserting a fact about a moving tree, and the assertion is cheapest to falsify at pickup. Related in kind to THR-614 (planned green-field over a system already built and dormant) — same root, opposite surface: there the plan under-read the tree, here the handoff over-read its own freshness. | Hourly `tb-opus-pickup` run, THR-734.
